# New Zealand

**New Zealand** ([Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language): *[Aotearoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aotearoa)*, pronounced [\[aɔˈtɛaɾɔa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/M%C4%81ori) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/00/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/00/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is an [island country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_country) in the southwestern [Pacific Ocean](23070.md). It comprises two main landmasses—the [North Island](62046.md) (*Te Ika-a-Māui*) and the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island) (*Te Waipounamu*)—and [over 700 smaller islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_New_Zealand). It is the [sixth-largest island country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_island_countries) by area and lies east of [Australia](4689264.md) across the [Tasman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Sea) and south of the islands of [New Caledonia](21342.md), [Fiji](10707.md), and [Tonga](30158.md). The [country's varied topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_New_Zealand) and sharp mountain peaks, including the [Southern Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alps) (*Kā Tiritiri o te Moana*), owe much to [tectonic uplift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tectonic_uplift) and volcanic eruptions. [New Zealand's capital city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_of_New_Zealand) is [Wellington](33804.md), and its most populous city is [Auckland](18660332.md).&#x20;

The islands of New Zealand were the last large habitable land to be settled by humans. Between about 1280 and 1350, [Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians) began to settle in the islands and subsequently developed a distinctive [Māori culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_culture). In 1642, the Dutch explorer [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman) became the first European to sight and record New Zealand. In 1769, the British explorer [Captain James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) became the first European to set foot on and map New Zealand. In 1840, representatives of the United Kingdom and [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) chiefs signed the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi), which paved the way for Britain's declaration of sovereignty later that year and the establishment of the [Colony of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_Zealand) in 1841. Subsequently, [a series of conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Wars) between the colonial government and [Māori tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) resulted in the [alienation and confiscation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_land_confiscations) of large amounts of Māori land. New Zealand [became a dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_Zealand) in 1907; it [gained full statutory independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_New_Zealand) in 1947, retaining the monarch as [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state). Today, the majority of [New Zealand's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealanders) of around 5.3 million is of [European descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_New_Zealanders); the indigenous Māori are the largest minority, followed by [Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_New_Zealanders) and [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders). Reflecting this, [New Zealand's culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Zealand) mainly derives from Māori and early [British](19097669.md) settlers but has recently broadened from increased [immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_New_Zealand). [The official languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_New_Zealand) are English, [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language), and [New Zealand Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Sign_Language), with the [local dialect of English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_English) being dominant.&#x20;

A [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), New Zealand was the first to introduce a [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage) and to give [women the right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage_in_New_Zealand). Recognised as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power), New Zealand [ranks very highly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_New_Zealand) in international measures of [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index) and [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_New_Zealand) and has one of the lowest levels of [perceived corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_perception_index) in the world. It retains [visible levels of inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality_in_New_Zealand), including [structural disparities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_discrimination_in_New_Zealand) between its Māori and European populations. During the 1980s, New Zealand underwent [major economic changes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogernomics) that transformed it from a [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) to a [liberalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization) [free-trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) economy. The [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy) dominates the [country's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_New_Zealand), followed by the industrial sector, and [agriculture](20243191.md); international [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_New_Zealand) is also a significant source of revenue. New Zealand and Australia have [a strong relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations) and are considered to share a strong [Trans-Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Tasman) identity, stemming from centuries of British colonisation. The country is part of [multiple international organisations and forums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outline_of_New_Zealand#International_organisation_membership).&#x20;

Nationally, legislative authority is vested in an elected, [unicameral](204304.md) [Parliament](340639.md), while executive political power is exercised by the [Government](8899161.md), led by the [prime minister](106321.md), currently [Christopher Luxon](62303596.md). [Charles III](125248.md) is [the country's king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_New_Zealand) and is represented by the [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand), [Cindy Kiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cindy_Kiro). New Zealand is organised into 11 [regional councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_New_Zealand) and 67 [territorial authorities](243907.md) for [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand) purposes. The [Realm of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realm_of_New_Zealand) also includes [Tokelau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokelau) (a [dependent territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependent_territory)); the [Cook Islands](7067.md) and [Niue](21228.md) (self-governing states in [free association](346414.md) with New Zealand); and the [Ross Dependency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Dependency), which is New Zealand's [territorial claim in Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_claims_in_Antarctica).&#x20;

## Etymology

Further information: [New Zealand place names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_place_names)

Brown square paper with Dutch writing and a thick red, curved line

Detail from a 1666 map showing the western coastline of *Nova Zeelandia* (on this map, north is at the bottom)

The first European visitor to New Zealand, Dutch explorer [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman), named the islands *Staten Land*, believing they were part of the *[Staten Landt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isla_de_los_Estados)* that [Jacob Le Maire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Le_Maire) had sighted off the southern end of South America.[^cite_note-StatenLandt-22][^cite_note-23] The next year, in 1643, [Hendrik Brouwer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hendrik_Brouwer) proved that the South American land was just a small island, and Dutch [cartographers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartographers) subsequently renamed Tasman's discovery *Nova Zeelandia* from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Latin), after the [Dutch province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_Zeeland) of [Zeeland](52229.md).[^cite_note-StatenLandt-22][^cite_note-24] This name was later [anglicised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicisation) to *New Zealand*.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-Names-26]&#x20;

This was written as *Nu Tireni* in the [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language) (spelt *Nu Tirani* in Te Tiriti o Waitangi). In 1834, a document written in Māori, "*He Wakaputanga o te Rangatiratanga o Nu Tireni*", was translated into English and became the [Declaration of the Independence of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Independence_of_New_Zealand). It was prepared by *Te W(h)akaminenga o Nga Rangatiratanga o Nga Hapu o Nu Tireni*, the [United Tribes of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Tribes_of_New_Zealand), and a copy was sent to King [William IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_IV), who had already acknowledged the flag of the United Tribes of New Zealand and who recognised the declaration in a letter from [Lord Glenelg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Grant,_1st_Baron_Glenelg).[^cite_note-glenelgletter-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalmer200841-28]&#x20;

*[Aotearoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aotearoa)* (pronounced Māori pronunciation: [\[aɔˈtɛaɾɔa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/M%C4%81ori) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/00/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/00/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> in Māori and [/ˌaʊtɛəˈroʊ.ə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) in English, often translated as 'land of the long white cloud')[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing200341-29] is the present Māori name for New Zealand; *Aotearoa* originally referred to just the [North Island](62046.md). Māori had no known name for the islands collectively before the arrival of Europeans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHayMaclaganGordon200872-30] Māori had several traditional names for the two main islands, including *Te Ika-a-Māui* ('the fish of [Māui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ui_\(M%C4%81ori_mythology\))') for the North Island and *Te Waipounamu* ('the waters of [greenstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounamu)') or *Te Waka o Aoraki* ('the canoe of [Aoraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aoraki)') for the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith20056-31] Early European maps labelled the islands North (North Island), Middle (South Island), and South ([Stewart Island / Rakiura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stewart_Island_/_Rakiura)).[^cite_note-32]&#x20;

In 1830, mapmakers began to use "North" and "South" on their maps to distinguish the two largest islands, and by 1907, this was the accepted norm.[^cite_note-Names-26] The [New Zealand Geographic Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Geographic_Board) discovered in 2009 that the names of the North Island and South Island had never been formalised, and names and alternative names were formalised in 2013. This set the names as *North Island* or *Te Ika-a-Māui*, and *South Island* or *Te Waipounamu*.[^cite_note-Williamson2013-33] For each island, either its English or Māori name can be used, or both can be used together.[^cite_note-Williamson2013-33] Similarly the Māori and English names for the whole country are sometimes used together (*Aotearoa New Zealand*);[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35] however, this has no official recognition.[^cite_note-36]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Zealand)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of New Zealand history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_New_Zealand_history).

One set of arrows point from Taiwan to Melanesia to Fiji/Samoa and then to the Marquesas Islands. The population then spread, some going south to New Zealand and others going north to Hawai'i. A second set start in southern Asia and end in Melanesia.

The [Māori people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) descend from [Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians) whose ancestors emigrated from [Taiwan](25734.md) to [Melanesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesia) between 3000 and 1000 [BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era) and then travelled east, reaching the [Society Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Islands) c. 1000 CE. After a pause of 200 to 300 years, a new wave of exploration led to the discovery and settlement of New Zealand.

[^cite_note-37]

[^cite_note-38]

[^cite_note-39]

### Early settlements

The first people to reach New Zealand were Polynesians in oceangoing *[waka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_\(canoe\))*, who are believed to have arrived in several waves between about 1280 and 1350. According to most [Māori oral traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_history), the islands were first discovered by the semi-legendary explorer [Kupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kupe) while in pursuit of a giant octopus.[^cite_note-40] These traditions hold that Kupe was followed by a great fleet of settlers, who set out from Hawaiki in eastern Polynesia around 1350.[^cite_note-41] The existence of a single great fleet that settled New Zealand has since been superseded by the belief that the majority of settlement was a planned and deliberate event that occurred over several decades.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46]&#x20;

The exact date of this settlement is unclear, with recent sources favouring settlement in the 14th century. While [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA) variability within Māori populations suggests that New Zealand was first settled between 1250 and 1300,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith20056-31][^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] no human remains, artefacts or structures can be reliably dated to earlier than the Kaharoa eruption of [Mount Tarawera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Tarawera) around 1314.[^cite_note-49] This scenario is also consistent with a debated third line of oral evidence,[^cite_note-Moon_2013-50] traditional genealogies (*[whakapapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whakapapa)*) that point to around 1350 as a probable arrival date for several of the [migratory waka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_migration_canoes) (canoes) from which many Māori trace their descent.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] Some Māori later migrated to the [Chatham Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands), where they developed their distinct [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori) culture;[^cite_note-53] a later 1835 invasion by Māori [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) resulted in the massacre and virtual extinction of the Moriori.[^cite_note-54]&#x20;

In a hostile 1642 encounter between [Ngāti Tūmatakōkiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_T%C5%ABmatak%C5%8Dkiri) and [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman)'s crew,[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith200523-56] four of Tasman's crew members were killed and at least one Māori was hit by [canister shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canister_shot).[^cite_note-57] Europeans did not revisit New Zealand until 1769, when British explorer [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) mapped almost the entire coastline.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith200523-56] Following Cook, New Zealand was visited by numerous European and North American [whaling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_whaling), [sealing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_hunting), and trading ships. They traded European food, metal tools, weapons, and other goods for timber, Māori food, artefacts, and water.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing2003122-58]&#x20;

### 19th century

The introduction of the [potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato) and the [musket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket) transformed Māori agriculture and warfare. Potatoes provided a reliable food surplus, which enabled longer and more sustained military campaigns.[^cite_note-59] The resulting intertribal [Musket Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket_Wars) encompassed over 600 battles between 1801 and 1840, killing 30,000–40,000 Māori.[^cite_note-60] From the early 19th century, Christian [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) began to settle New Zealand, eventually [converting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion) most of the Māori population.[^cite_note-61] The Māori population declined to around 40% of its pre-contact level during the 19th century; introduced diseases were the major factor.[^cite_note-62]&#x20;

A torn sheet of paper

The Waitangi sheet from the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi)

The [British Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resident_minister) appointed [James Busby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Busby) as British Resident to New Zealand in 1832.[^cite_note-Busby-63] His duties, given to him by Governor [Bourke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Bourke) in Sydney, were to protect settlers and traders "of good standing", prevent "outrages" against Māori, and apprehend escaped convicts.[^cite_note-Busby-63][^cite_note-64] In 1835, following an announcement of impending French settlement by [Charles de Thierry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Thierry), the nebulous [United Tribes of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Tribes_of_New_Zealand) sent [a Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/He_Whakaputanga) to King [William IV of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_IV_of_the_United_Kingdom) asking for protection.[^cite_note-Busby-63] The [Colonial Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Office) sent Captain [William Hobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hobson) to claim British sovereignty and negotiate a treaty with the Māori, prompted by growing unrest, the [New Zealand Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Company)'s land-buying survey, and the legal ambiguity of the 1835 Declaration of Independence.[^cite_note-65] The [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) was first signed in the [Bay of Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Islands) on 6 February 1840.[^cite_note-Wilson2009-66] In response to the New Zealand Company's attempts to establish an independent settlement in [Wellington](33804.md),[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] Hobson declared British sovereignty over all of New Zealand on 21 May 1840, even though copies of the treaty were still circulating throughout the country for Māori to sign.[^cite_note-69] With the signing of the treaty and declaration of sovereignty, the number of immigrants, particularly from the United Kingdom, began to increase.[^cite_note-70]&#x20;

New Zealand was administered as a dependency of the [Colony of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_South_Wales) until becoming a separate [Crown colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_colony), the [Colony of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_Zealand), on 3 May 1841.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] Armed conflict began between the colonial government and Māori in 1843 with the [Wairau Affray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairau_Affray) over land and disagreements over sovereignty. These conflicts, mainly in the North Island, saw thousands of imperial troops and the Royal Navy come to New Zealand, and became known as the [New Zealand Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Wars). Following these armed conflicts, large areas of [Māori land were confiscated by the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_land_confiscations).[^cite_note-73]&#x20;

Black and white engraving depicting a crowd of people

A meeting of European and Māori residents of [Hawke's Bay Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawke%27s_Bay_Province). Engraving, 1863.

The colony gained a [representative government in 1852](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Constitution_Act_1852), and the [first Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_New_Zealand_Parliament) met in 1854.[^cite_note-G_and_N-74] In 1856 the colony effectively became self-governing, gaining responsibility over all domestic matters, except [native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples) policy, which was granted in the mid-1860s.[^cite_note-G_and_N-74] Following concerns that the South Island might form a separate colony, premier [Alfred Domett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Domett) moved a resolution to transfer the [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_of_New_Zealand) from Auckland to a locality near [Cook Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Strait).[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] Wellington was chosen for its central location, with Parliament officially sitting there for the first time in 1865.[^cite_note-77]&#x20;

In 1886, following requests from the government in Wellington concerned about foreign influence in the region, the Secretary of State for Colonies in London reluctantly agreed to annex the volcanic [Kermadec Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermadec_Islands) to New Zealand, islands that were seemingly not of "any possible use...to that colony". As a result, instructions were issued directing the Admiral on the Australian Station to hoist the British flag on the Kermadecs,[^cite_note-78] which lie about 1,000 km (620 mi) northeast of Auckland. Since 1937, the islands have been uninhabited except for about six people at [Raoul Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_Island) station. These islands put the northern border of New Zealand at 29 degrees South latitude.[^cite_note-ENZ1966-79] After the 1982 [UNCLOS](31940.md), the islands contributed significantly to New Zealand's [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-80]&#x20;

In 1891, the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Liberal_Party) came to power as the first organised political party.[^cite_note-Liberal-81] The [Liberal Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Government_of_New_Zealand), led by [Richard Seddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Seddon) for most of its period in office,[^cite_note-82] passed many important social and economic measures. In 1893, New Zealand was the first nation in the world to grant all [women the right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage_in_New_Zealand)[^cite_note-Liberal-81] and pioneered the [adoption of compulsory arbitration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Conciliation_and_Arbitration_Act_1894) between employers and unions in 1894.[^cite_note-83] The Liberals also guaranteed a minimum wage in 1894, a world first.[^cite_note-84]&#x20;



Residents of [Dunedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunedin) celebrate the news of the [Armistice of 11 November 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918).

### 20th century

In 1907, at the request of the New Zealand Parliament, King [Edward VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_VII) proclaimed New Zealand a [Dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_Zealand) within the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire),[^cite_note-85] reflecting its self-governing status.[^cite_note-86] At the [1926 Imperial Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_Imperial_Conference), the Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom and the Dominions [unanimously declared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1926) that all were equal and autonomous members of the British Commonwealth of Nations. In 1947, New Zealand [adopted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_Adoption_Act_1947) the [Statute of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931) implementing the declaration, confirming that the [British Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Parliament) could no longer legislate for the country without its consent. The British government's residual legislative powers were later removed by the [Constitution Act 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Act_1986), and final rights of appeal to the [British Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Privy_Council) were abolished in 2003.[^cite_note-G_and_N-74]&#x20;

Early in the 20th century, New Zealand was involved in world affairs, fighting in the [First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_New_Zealand_during_World_War_I) and [Second World Wars](607552.md)[^cite_note-87] and suffering through the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression).[^cite_note-88] The depression led to the election of the [first Labour Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Labour_Government_of_New_Zealand) and the establishment of a comprehensive [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) and a [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist) economy.[^cite_note-89] New Zealand experienced increasing prosperity following the Second World War,[^cite_note-90] and Māori began to leave their traditional rural life and move to the cities in search of work.[^cite_note-91] A [Māori protest movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_protest_movement) developed, which criticised [Eurocentrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocentrism) and worked for greater recognition of [Māori culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_culture) and of the Treaty of Waitangi.[^cite_note-92] In 1975, the [Waitangi Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Tribunal) was set up to investigate alleged breaches of the Treaty, and it was enabled to investigate historic grievances in 1985.[^cite_note-Wilson2009-66] The government has negotiated [settlements of these grievances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi_claims_and_settlements) with many iwi,[^cite_note-93] although [Māori claims to the foreshore and seabed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_foreshore_and_seabed_controversy) proved controversial in the 2000s.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95]&#x20;

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_New_Zealand) and [Environment of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_New_Zealand)

Islands of New Zealand as seen from satellite

The snow-capped [Southern Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alps) dominate the South Island, while the North Island's [Northland Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northland_Peninsula) stretches towards the subtropics.

New Zealand is near the centre of the [water hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_hemisphere) and comprises two main islands and [more than 700 smaller islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-Walrond1-96] The two main islands (the [North Island](62046.md), or *Te Ika-a-Māui*, and the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island), or *Te Waipounamu*) are separated by [Cook Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Strait), 22 kilometres (14 mi) wide at its narrowest point.[^cite_note-97] Besides the North and South Islands, the five largest inhabited islands are [Stewart Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stewart_Island) (across the [Foveaux Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foveaux_Strait)), [Chatham Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Island), [Great Barrier Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Barrier_Island) (in the [Hauraki Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauraki_Gulf)),[^cite_note-98] [D'Urville Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%27Urville_Island_\(New_Zealand\)) (in the [Marlborough Sounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlborough_Sounds))[^cite_note-99] and [Waiheke Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waiheke_Island) (about 22 km (14 mi) from central Auckland).[^cite_note-100] The nearest continent is [Australia](4689264.md) to the west north west, with the shortest distance between the Australian and New Zealand mainlands being roughly 1,487 kilometers (924 miles) across the [Tasman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Sea), specifically between [Tasmania](29944.md) and [Fiordland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiordland). The closest point between any territory of both countries is between [Macquarie Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macquarie_Island) (Australia) and [Auckland Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Island) (New Zealand), which are about 618 kilometers (384 miles) apart.&#x20;

New Zealand is long and narrow—over 1,600 kilometres (990 mi) along its north-north-east axis with a maximum width of 400 kilometres (250 mi)[^cite_note-101]—with about 15,000 km (9,300 mi) of coastline[^cite_note-CIA-102] and a total land area of 268,000 square kilometres (103,500 sq mi).[^cite_note-103] Because of its far-flung outlying islands and long coastline, the country has extensive marine resources. Its [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_New_Zealand) is one of the world's largest, covering more than 15 times its land area.[^cite_note-104]&#x20;



[Aoraki / Mount Cook](20212.md) is the highest point in New Zealand, at 3,724 metres.

The South Island is the largest landmass of New Zealand. It is divided along its length by the [Southern Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alps).[^cite_note-105] There are 18 peaks over 3,000 metres (9,800 ft), the highest of which is [Aoraki / Mount Cook](20212.md) at 3,724 metres (12,218 ft).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarden200552-106] [Fiordland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiordland)'s steep mountains and deep [fiords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fiords_of_New_Zealand) record the extensive ice age glaciation of this southwestern corner of the South Island.[^cite_note-107] The North Island is less mountainous but is [marked by volcanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanology_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-108] The highly active [Taupō Volcanic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taup%C5%8D_Volcanic_Zone) has formed a large [volcanic plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Island_Volcanic_Plateau), punctuated by the North Island's highest mountain, [Mount Ruapehu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Ruapehu) (2,797 metres (9,177 ft)). The plateau also hosts the country's largest lake, [Lake Taupō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Taup%C5%8D),[^cite_note-Walrond1-96] nestled in the [caldera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caldera) of one of the world's most active [supervolcanoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervolcano).[^cite_note-109] New Zealand is prone to [earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_New_Zealand).&#x20;

The country owes its varied topography, and perhaps even its emergence above the waves, to the dynamic boundary it straddles between the [Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Plate) and [Indo-Australian Plates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Australian_Plate).[^cite_note-Keith_2009-110] New Zealand is part of [Zealandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealandia), a [microcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microcontinent) nearly half the size of Australia that gradually submerged after breaking away from the [Gondwanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana) supercontinent.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] About 25 million years ago, a shift in [plate tectonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonic) movements began to [contort and crumple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaikoura_orogeny) the region. This is now most evident in the Southern Alps, formed by [compression of the crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_collision) beside the [Alpine Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_Fault). Elsewhere, the plate boundary involves the [subduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subduction) of one plate under the other, producing the [Puysegur Trench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puysegur_Trench) to the south, the [Hikurangi Trough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hikurangi_Trough) east of the North Island, and the [Kermadec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermadec_Trench) and [Tonga Trenches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonga_Trench)[^cite_note-113] further north.[^cite_note-Keith_2009-110]&#x20;

New Zealand, together with Australia, is part of a wider region known as [Australasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasia).[^cite_note-NZOD-114] It also forms the southwestern extremity of the geographic and ethnographic region called [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia).[^cite_note-115] Oceania is a wider region encompassing the [Australian continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_\(continent\)), New Zealand, and various island countries in the Pacific Ocean that are not included in the [seven-continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent) model.[^cite_note-116]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_New_Zealand)



[Köppen-Geiger climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen-Geiger_climate_classification) map of New Zealand



Autumn in the [Christchurch Botanic Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch_Botanic_Gardens)

New Zealand's climate is predominantly temperate [maritime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): Cfb), with mean annual temperatures ranging from 10 °C (50 °F) in the south to 16 °C (61 °F) in the north.[^cite_note-Mullan2009-117] Historical [maxima and minima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxima_and_minima) are 42.4 °C (108.32 °F) in [Rangiora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangiora), [Canterbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canterbury_Region) and −25.6 °C (−14.08 °F) in [Ranfurly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranfurly,_New_Zealand), [Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otago).[^cite_note-niwa-118] Conditions vary sharply across regions, from extremely wet on the [West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast,_New_Zealand) of the South Island to [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate) in [Central Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Otago) and the [Mackenzie Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mackenzie_Basin) of inland Canterbury and [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropical) in [Northland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northland_Peninsula).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]&#x20;

Of the seven largest cities, [Christchurch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch) is the driest, receiving on average only 618 millimetres (24.3 in) of rain per year. Wellington is the wettest, receiving almost twice that amount.[^cite_note-121] Auckland, Wellington and Christchurch all receive a yearly average of more than 2,000 hours of sunshine. The southern and southwestern parts of the South Island have a cooler and cloudier climate, with around 1,400–1,600 hours. The northern and northeastern parts of the South Island are the sunniest areas of the country and receive about 2,400–2,500 hours.[^cite_note-122]&#x20;

Snow generally falls from June to October, although [cold snaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_snap) can occur outside of those months.[^cite_note-123] Snowfall is common in the eastern and southern parts of the South Island and mountain areas across the country.[^cite_note-Mullan2009-117]&#x20;
