### Biodiversity

Main article: [Biodiversity of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_of_New_Zealand)

Kiwi among sticks

The endemic flightless [kiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwi_\(bird\)) is a [national icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_New_Zealand).

New Zealand's [geographic isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographic_isolation) for 80 million years[^cite_note-125] and island [biogeography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogeography) has influenced evolution of the country's species of [animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_New_Zealand), [fungi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fungi_of_New_Zealand) and [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_of_New_Zealand). Physical isolation has caused biological isolation, resulting in a dynamic evolutionary ecology with examples of distinctive plants and animals as well as populations of widespread species.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] The flora and fauna of New Zealand were originally thought to have originated from New Zealand's fragmentation off from Gondwana; however, more recent evidence postulates species resulted from dispersal.[^cite_note-128]&#x20;

About 82% of New Zealand's indigenous [vascular plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vascular_plant) are [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemism), covering 1,944 species across 65 [genera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genus).[^cite_note-FAQ-129][^cite_note-NZPCN_2006-130] The number of fungi recorded from New Zealand, including lichen-forming species, is not known, nor is the proportion of those fungi which are endemic, but one estimate suggests there are about 2,300 species of lichen-forming fungi in New Zealand[^cite_note-FAQ-129] and 40% of these are endemic.[^cite_note-131] The two main types of forest are those dominated by broadleaf trees with emergent [podocarps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podocarp), or by [southern beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nothofagus) in cooler climates.[^cite_note-132] The remaining vegetation types consist of grasslands, the majority of which are [tussock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tussock_grasslands_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-133]&#x20;

An artist's rendition of a Haast's eagle attacking two moa

The giant [Haast's eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haast%27s_eagle) died out when Māori hunted its main prey, the [moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moa), to extinction.

Before humans arrived, an estimated 80% of the land was covered in forest, with only [high alpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_line), wet, infertile and volcanic areas without trees.[^cite_note-134] Massive [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_New_Zealand) occurred after humans arrived, with around half the forest cover lost to fire after Polynesian settlement.[^cite_note-135] Much of the remaining forest fell after European settlement, being logged or cleared to make room for pastoral farming, leaving forest occupying only 23% of the land in 1997.[^cite_note-136]&#x20;

The forests were dominated by [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birds_of_New_Zealand), and the lack of mammalian predators led to some, like the [kiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwi_\(bird\)), [kākāpō](89074.md), [weka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weka) and [takahē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takah%C4%93), evolving [flightlessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flightless_bird).[^cite_note-137] The arrival of humans, associated changes to habitat, and the introduction of [rats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_rat), ferrets and other mammals led to the [extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Zealand_animals_extinct_in_the_Holocene) of many bird species, including [large birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megafauna) like the [moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moa) and [Haast's eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haast%27s_eagle).[^cite_note-Holdaway2009-138][^cite_note-139] The forests of the country are home to many endemic birds including [kererū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerer%C5%AB), [New Zealand falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_falcon), [kākā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81k%C4%81), [red-crowned parakeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red-crowned_parakeet), [rifleman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifleman_\(bird\)), [tūī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%AB%C4%AB), [New Zealand fantail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_fantail), [kōkako](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dkako), [saddleback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddleback_\(bird\)), [stitchbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stitchbird), and [New Zealand fernbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_fernbird).[^cite_note-140] The shorelines are inhabited by many endemic shorebirds including [black stilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_stilt), [variable oystercatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variable_oystercatcher), [shore plover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shore_plover), [spotted shag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotted_shag), and [New Zealand king shag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_king_shag). Due to the isolation of the islands and historic lack of predators, the nation is home to countless breeding seabirds including [black-billed gull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-billed_gull), [northern royal albatross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_royal_albatross), [white-faced storm petrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-faced_storm_petrel), [grey-faced petrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey-faced_petrel), [fairy prion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairy_prion), and [Australasian gannet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasian_gannet).[^cite_note-141]&#x20;

Other indigenous animals are represented by reptiles ([tuatara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuatara), [skinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skink) and [geckos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_geckos_of_New_Zealand)), frogs,[^cite_note-142] such as the protected endangered [Hamilton's Frog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton%27s_Frog), [spiders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiders_of_New_Zealand),[^cite_note-143] insects ([wētā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C4%93t%C4%81)),[^cite_note-144] and snails.[^cite_note-145] Some, such as the tuatara, are so unique that they have been called [living fossils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_fossil).[^cite_note-146] Three species of bats ([one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_greater_short-tailed_bat) since extinct) were the only sign of native land mammals in New Zealand until the 2006 discovery of bones from [a unique, mouse-sized land mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Bathans_mammal) at least 16 million years old.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] Marine mammals are abundant, with almost half the world's [cetaceans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cetacea) (whales, dolphins, and [porpoises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porpoise)) and large numbers of [fur seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_seal) reported in New Zealand waters.[^cite_note-149] Many seabirds breed in New Zealand, a third of them unique to the country.[^cite_note-150] More [penguin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_birds_of_New_Zealand#Penguins) species are found in New Zealand than in any other country, with 13 of the world's 18 penguin species.[^cite_note-151]&#x20;

Since human arrival, almost half of the country's vertebrate species have become extinct, including at least 51 birds, three frogs, three lizards, one freshwater fish, and one bat. Others are endangered or have had their range severely reduced.[^cite_note-Holdaway2009-138] New Zealand conservationists have pioneered several methods to help threatened wildlife recover, including island sanctuaries, pest control, wildlife translocation, fostering, and ecological [restoration of islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_restoration) and other [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155]&#x20;



## Government and politics

Main articles: [New Zealand Government](8899161.md) and [Politics of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_New_Zealand)

See also: [International rankings of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_New_Zealand)

The King wearing a pinstripe suit

[Charles III](125248.md),\
&#x20;[King of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_New_Zealand)

A woman wearing medals

[Cindy Kiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cindy_Kiro), [Governor-General of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand)

A smiling man wearing a dark business suit and tie

[Christopher Luxon](62303596.md), [Prime Minister of New Zealand](106321.md)

New Zealand is a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) with a [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy),[^cite_note-GG_constitution-156] although [its constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Zealand) is [not codified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncodified_constitution).[^cite_note-Economist_factsheet-157] [Charles III](125248.md) is the [King of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_New_Zealand)[^cite_note-158] and thus the [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state).[^cite_note-159] The king is represented by the [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand), whom he appoints on the [advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_\(constitutional\)) of the [prime minister](106321.md).[^cite_note-160] The governor-general can exercise [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown)'s [prerogative powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prerogative_powers), such as reviewing cases of injustice and making appointments of [ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministers_in_the_New_Zealand_Government), ambassadors, and other key public officials,[^cite_note-161] and in rare situations, the [reserve powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_power) (e.g. the power to dissolve Parliament or refuse the [royal assent](153067.md) of a [bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_\(law\)) into law).[^cite_note-reserve_Powers-162] The powers of the monarch and the governor-general are limited by constitutional constraints and cannot normally be exercised without the advice of ministers.[^cite_note-reserve_Powers-162]&#x20;

The [New Zealand Parliament](340639.md) holds [legislative power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_power) and consists of the king and the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_House_of_Representatives).[^cite_note-parliament_facts-163] It also included an upper house, the [Legislative Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Legislative_Council), until this was abolished in 1950.[^cite_note-parliament_facts-163] The [supremacy of parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacy_of_parliament) over the Crown and other government institutions was established in England by the [Bill of Rights 1689](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_of_Rights_1689) and has been ratified as law in New Zealand.[^cite_note-parliament_facts-163] The House of Representatives is democratically elected, and a government is formed from the party or [coalition](6036.md) with the majority of seats. If no majority is formed, a [minority government](271434.md) can be formed if support from other parties during [confidence and supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_and_supply) votes is assured.[^cite_note-parliament_facts-163] The governor-general appoints ministers under advice from the prime minister, who is by [convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_convention_\(political_custom\)) the [parliamentary leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_leader) of the governing party or coalition.[^cite_note-164] [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_New_Zealand), formed by ministers and led by the prime minister, is the highest policy-making body in government and responsible for deciding significant government actions.[^cite_note-165] Members of Cabinet make major decisions collectively and are therefore [collectively responsible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_collective_responsibility) for the consequences of these decisions.[^cite_note-166] The 42nd and current prime minister, since 27 November 2023, is [Christopher Luxon](62303596.md).[^cite_note-167]&#x20;

A block of buildings fronted by a large statue.

A statue of [Richard Seddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Seddon), the "[Beehive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beehive_\(New_Zealand\))" (Executive Wing), and [Parliament House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_House,_Wellington) (right), in Parliament Grounds, [Wellington](33804.md)

A [parliamentary general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_New_Zealand) must be called no later than three years after the previous election.[^cite_note-168] Almost all general elections between [1853](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1853_New_Zealand_general_election) and [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_New_Zealand_general_election) were held under the [first-past-the-post voting](261709.md) system.[^cite_note-169] Since the [1996 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_New_Zealand_general_election), a form of [proportional representation](23298.md) called [mixed-member proportional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-member_proportional) (MMP) has been used.[^cite_note-Economist_factsheet-157] Under the MMP system, each person has two votes; one is for a candidate standing in the voter's [electorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_electorates), and the other is for a party. Based on the 2018 census data, there are 72 electorates (which include seven [Māori electorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_electorates) in which only Māori can optionally vote),[^cite_note-170] and the remaining 48 of the 120 seats are assigned so that representation in Parliament reflects the party vote, with the threshold that a party must win at least one electorate or 5% of the total party vote before it is eligible for a seat.[^cite_note-171] Elections since the 1930s have been dominated by two [political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_New_Zealand), [National](333149.md) and [Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Labour_Party). More parties have been represented in Parliament since the introduction of MMP.[^cite_note-172]&#x20;

[New Zealand's judiciary](41422695.md), headed by the [chief justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_New_Zealand),[^cite_note-173] includes the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_New_Zealand), [Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_of_New_Zealand), the [High Court](3902269.md), and subordinate courts.[^cite_note-174] Judges and judicial officers are appointed non-politically and under strict rules regarding tenure to help maintain [judicial independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence).[^cite_note-Economist_factsheet-157] This theoretically allows the judiciary to interpret the law based solely on the legislation enacted by Parliament without other influences on their decisions.[^cite_note-175]&#x20;

New Zealand is identified as one of the world's most stable and well-governed states.[^cite_note-176] As of 2023, the country is ranked second in the strength of its democratic institutions,[^cite_note-177] and third in government transparency and [lack of corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-178] [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_New_Zealand) in the nation are also recognised as among the most tolerant in Oceania.[^cite_note-179] New Zealand ranks highly for civic participation in the political process, with 82% [voter turnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_turnout) during recent general elections, compared to an [OECD](33853117.md) average of 69%.[^cite_note-180] But this is untrue of local council elections; a historically low 36% of eligible New Zealanders voted in the [2022 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_New_Zealand_local_elections), compared with an already low 42% turnout in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_New_Zealand_local_elections).[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] A 2017 [human rights report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_Reports_on_Human_Rights_Practices) by the [United States Department of State](31975.md) noted that the New Zealand government generally [respected the rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_New_Zealand) of individuals, but voiced concerns regarding the social status of the Māori population.[^cite_note-184] The [New Zealand Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Commission) has asserted that there is strong, consistent evidence that [structural discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_discrimination_in_New_Zealand) is a real and ongoing socioeconomic issue.[^cite_note-185] One example of structural inequality in New Zealand can be seen in the criminal justice system. According to the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(New_Zealand\)), Māori are overrepresented, comprising 45% of New Zealanders convicted of crimes and 53% of those imprisoned, while only being 16.5% of the population.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]&#x20;

### Regions and external territories

Main articles: [Regions of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_New_Zealand) and [Realm of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realm_of_New_Zealand)

A map of New Zealand divided into regions and territorial authorities with labels

Map of regions (coloured) and territorial authorities (outlined) in New Zealand

Under legislation passed in London, New Zealand was divided into six [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_New_Zealand) in 1853. Following further legislation shortly afterwards three further provinces were created from three of the original six, leaving the country with nine provinces in 1862, each with a degree of autonomy under an elected [superintendent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superintendent_\(New_Zealand\)).[^cite_note-nine_provinces-188] Because of financial pressures and the desire to consolidate railways, education, land sales, and other policies, government was centralised and the provinces were abolished in 1876.[^cite_note-189] The provinces are remembered in [regional public holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_New_Zealand)[^cite_note-190] and sporting rivalries.[^cite_note-191]&#x20;

Since 1876, various councils have administered local areas under legislation determined by the central government.[^cite_note-nine_provinces-188][^cite_note-192] In 1989, the government reorganised [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand) into the current two-tier structure of [regional councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_New_Zealand) and [territorial authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_authorities).[^cite_note-Sancton2000-193] The [249 municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_former_territorial_authorities_in_New_Zealand)[^cite_note-Sancton2000-193] that existed in 1975 have been consolidated into 67 territorial authorities and 11 regional councils.[^cite_note-194] The regional councils' role is to regulate "the natural environment with particular emphasis on [resource management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991)",[^cite_note-Sancton2000-193] while territorial authorities are responsible for sewage, water, local roads, building consents, and other local matters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmeltJui_Lin200933-195][^cite_note-glossary-196] Five of the territorial councils are [unitary authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authority) and also act as regional councils.[^cite_note-glossary-196] The territorial authorities consist of 13 city councils, 53 [district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_New_Zealand) councils, and the [Chatham Islands Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands_Council). While officially the Chatham Islands Council is not a unitary authority, it undertakes many functions of a regional council.[^cite_note-197]&#x20;

The Realm of New Zealand, one of 15 [Commonwealth realms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_realm),[^cite_note-198] is the entire area over which the king or queen of New Zealand is [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign) and comprises New Zealand, [Tokelau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokelau), the [Ross Dependency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Dependency), the [Cook Islands](7067.md), and [Niue](21228.md).[^cite_note-GG_constitution-156] The Cook Islands and Niue are self-governing states in [free association](346414.md) with New Zealand.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] The New Zealand Parliament cannot pass legislation for these countries, but with their consent can act on behalf of them in foreign affairs and defence. Tokelau is classified as a [non-self-governing territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_list_of_non-self-governing_territories), but is administered by a council of three elders (one from each Tokelauan [atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoll)).[^cite_note-201] The Ross Dependency is New Zealand's [territorial claim in Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_claims_in_Antarctica), where it operates the [Scott Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Base) research facility.[^cite_note-202] [New Zealand nationality law](2316836.md) treats all parts of the realm equally, so most people born in New Zealand, the Cook Islands, Niue, Tokelau, and the Ross Dependency are New Zealand citizens.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-205]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_New_Zealand)



Former New Zealand prime minister [Jacinda Ardern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacinda_Ardern) with US president [Joe Biden](145422.md) in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office), 2022

&#x20;During the period of the New Zealand colony, Britain was responsible for external trade and foreign relations.[^cite_note-206] The 1923 and 1926 [Imperial Conferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Conference) decided that New Zealand should be allowed to negotiate its own political [treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaties), and the first commercial treaty was ratified in 1928 with Japan. On 3 September 1939, New Zealand allied itself with Britain and [declared war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war) on Germany with Prime Minister [Michael Joseph Savage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Joseph_Savage) proclaiming, "Where she goes, we go; where she stands, we stand".[^cite_note-207]

A squad of men kneel in the desert sand while performing a war dance

[Māori Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Battalion) [haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka) in Egypt, 1941

In 1951, New Zealand, Australia, and the US signed the ANZUS Treaty.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] The influence of the United States on New Zealand weakened following protests over the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_in_the_Vietnam_War),[^cite_note-210] the refusal of the United States to admonish France after the [sinking of the *Rainbow Warrior*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinking_of_the_Rainbow_Warrior),[^cite_note-211] disagreements over environmental and agricultural trade issues, and [New Zealand's nuclear-free policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_nuclear-free_zone).[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] Despite the United States's suspension of ANZUS obligations, the treaty remained in effect between New Zealand and Australia, whose foreign policy has followed a similar historical trend.[^cite_note-214] Close political contact is maintained between the two countries, with [free trade agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closer_Economic_Relations) and [travel arrangements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Tasman_Travel_Arrangement) that allow citizens to visit, live and work in both countries without restrictions.[^cite_note-NZ_in_brief-215] In 2013 there were about 650,000 New Zealand citizens living in Australia, which is equivalent to 15% of the population of New Zealand.[^cite_note-216]&#x20;

New Zealand has a strong presence among the [Pacific Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Island) countries, and enjoys strong diplomatic relations with [Samoa](27238.md), [Fiji](10707.md), and [Tonga](30158.md), and among smaller nations.[^cite_note-217] A large proportion of New Zealand's aid goes to these countries, and many Pacific people migrate to New Zealand for employment. The increase of this since the 1960s led to the formation of the [Pasifika New Zealander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders) pan-ethnic group, the fourth-largest ethnic grouping in the country.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] Permanent migration is regulated under the 1970 Samoan Quota Scheme and the 2002 Pacific Access Category, which allow up to 1,100 Samoan nationals and up to 750 other Pacific Islanders respectively to become permanent New Zealand residents each year. A seasonal workers scheme for temporary migration was introduced in 2007, and in 2009 about 8,000 Pacific Islanders were employed under it.[^cite_note-220] New Zealand is involved in the [Pacific Islands Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islands_Forum), the [Pacific Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Community), [Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation), and the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) Regional Forum (including the [East Asia Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia_Summit)).[^cite_note-NZ_in_brief-215] New Zealand has been described as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) in the Asia-Pacific region,[^cite_note-221] and an [emerging power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_power).[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] The country is a member of the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_and_the_United_Nations),[^cite_note-224] the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations)[^cite_note-225] and the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) (OECD),[^cite_note-226] and participates in the [Five Power Defence Arrangements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Power_Defence_Arrangements).[^cite_note-227]&#x20;

Today, New Zealand enjoys [particularly close relations](12792048.md) with the United States and is one of its [major non-NATO allies](3812445.md),[^cite_note-\:0-228] as [well as with Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations), with a "[Trans-Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Tasman)" identity between citizens of the latter being common.[^cite_note-Lynch2009-229] New Zealand is a member of the [Five Eyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Eyes) intelligence sharing agreement, known formally as the [UKUSA Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UKUSA_Agreement). The five members of this agreement comprise the [core Anglosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglosphere): Australia, Canada, New Zealand, the United Kingdom, and the United States.[^cite_note-230] Since 2012, New Zealand has had a partnership arrangement with [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization) under the Partnership Interoperability Initiative.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] According to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index), New Zealand is the 4th most peaceful country in the world.[^cite_note-234]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [New Zealand Defence Force](21359.md)

A soldier in a green army uniform faces forward

[Anzac Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anzac_Day) service at the National War Memorial

New Zealand's military services—the New Zealand Defence Force—comprise the [New Zealand Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Army), the [Royal New Zealand Air Force](190903.md) and the [Royal New Zealand Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_New_Zealand_Navy).[^cite_note-235] New Zealand's [national defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_defence) needs are modest since a direct attack is unlikely.[^cite_note-236] However, its military has [had a global presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_New_Zealand). The country fought in both world wars, with notable campaigns in [Gallipoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallipoli_Campaign), [Crete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Crete),[^cite_note-237] [El Alamein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_El_Alamein),[^cite_note-238] and [Monte Cassino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Monte_Cassino).[^cite_note-239] The Gallipoli campaign played an important part in fostering New Zealand's [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity)[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] and strengthened the [ANZAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_and_New_Zealand_Army_Corps) tradition it shares with Australia.[^cite_note-242]&#x20;

In addition the two world wars, New Zealand fought in the [Second Boer War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Boer_War),[^cite_note-243] the [Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_in_the_Korean_War),[^cite_note-244] the [Malayan Emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Emergency),[^cite_note-245] the [Vietnam War](32611.md), the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), and the [Afghanistan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)). It has contributed forces to several regional and global peacekeeping missions, such as those in [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprus_dispute), [Somalia](3070332.md), [Bosnia and Herzegovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War), the [Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis), [Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angolan_Civil_War), [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War), the [Iran–Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) border, [Bougainville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bougainville_campaign), [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Astute), and the [Solomon Islands](265083.md#Civil_war).[^cite_note-246]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_New_Zealand)

See also: [List of companies of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_of_New_Zealand)

Boats docked in blue-green water. Plate glass skyscrapers rising up in the background.

[Waterfront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_waterfront) along [Auckland CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_CBD), a major hub of economic activity

New Zealand has an [advanced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_economy) [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy),[^cite_note-247] ranked 16th in the 2022 [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index),[^cite_note-248] and fourth in the 2022 [Index of Economic Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom).[^cite_note-249] It is a [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) with a [nominal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_value) gross domestic product (GDP) per capita (in United States dollar equivalents) of \$48,071 as of 2023.[^cite_note-IMFWEO.NZ-15]The currency is the [New Zealand dollar](161585.md), informally known as the "Kiwi dollar"; it also circulates in the Cook Islands (see [Cook Islands dollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands_dollar)), Niue, Tokelau, and the [Pitcairn Islands](23450.md).[^cite_note-250]&#x20;

Historically, extractive industries have contributed strongly to New Zealand's economy, focusing at different times on sealing, whaling, [flax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phormium), gold, [kauri gum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kauri_gum), and native timber.[^cite_note-RWT_export_evolution-251] The first shipment of refrigerated meat on the *[Dunedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunedin_\(1874_ship\))* in 1882 led to the establishment of meat and dairy exports to Britain, a trade which provided the basis for strong economic growth in New Zealand.[^cite_note-252] High demand for agricultural products from the United Kingdom and the United States helped New Zealanders achieve higher living standards than both Australia and Western Europe in the 1950s and 1960s.[^cite_note-253] In 1973, New Zealand's export market was reduced when the United Kingdom joined the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community)[^cite_note-254] and other compounding factors, such as the [1973 oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis) and [1979 energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_oil_crisis) crises, led to a severe [economic depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression_\(economics\)).[^cite_note-255] Living standards in New Zealand fell behind those of Australia and Western Europe, and by 1982 New Zealand had the lowest per-capita income of all the developed nations surveyed by [the World Bank](36752.md).[^cite_note-256] In the mid-1980s New Zealand deregulated [its agricultural sector](20243191.md) by phasing out [subsidies](171866.md) over a three-year period.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] Since 1984, successive governments engaged in major [macroeconomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macroeconomic) restructuring (known first as [Rogernomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogernomics) and then [Ruthanasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthanasia)), rapidly transforming New Zealand from a [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) and highly regulated economy to a liberalised [free-trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-trade) economy.[^cite_note-Liberalisation-259][^cite_note-260]&#x20;

Blue water against a backdrop of snow-capped mountains

[Milford Sound / Piopiotahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milford_Sound_/_Piopiotahi) is one of New Zealand's most famous tourist destinations.

[^cite_note-261]

Unemployment peaked just above 10% in 1991 and 1992,[^cite_note-unemployment-262] following the [1987 share market crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Monday_\(1987\)#New_Zealand), but eventually fell to 3.7% in 2007 (ranking third from twenty-seven comparable OECD nations).[^cite_note-unemployment-262] However, the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) had a major effect on New Zealand, with the GDP shrinking for five consecutive quarters, the longest recession in over thirty years,[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264] and unemployment rising back to 7% in late 2009.[^cite_note-265] The lowest unemployment rate recorded using the current methodology was in December 2021 during the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), at 3.2%.[^cite_note-266] Unemployment rates for different age groups follow similar trends but are consistently higher among youth. During the September 2025 quarter, the general unemployment rate was around 5.3%, while the unemployment rate for youth aged 15 to 24 was 15.2%.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-unemployment-262][^cite_note-268] New Zealand has experienced a series of "[brain drains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight)" since the 1970s[^cite_note-269] that still continue today.[^cite_note-270] Nearly one-quarter of highly skilled workers live overseas, mostly in Australia and Britain, which is the largest proportion from any developed nation.[^cite_note-271] In recent decades, however, a "[brain gain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight)" has brought in educated professionals from Europe and less developed countries.[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain200644-273] Today New Zealand's economy benefits from a high level of [innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation).[^cite_note-274]&#x20;

[Poverty in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_New_Zealand) is characterised by growing income inequality; wealth in New Zealand [is highly concentrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality_in_New_Zealand),[^cite_note-275] with the top 1% of the population owning 16% of the country's wealth, and the richest 5% owning 38%, leaving a stark contrast where half the population, including [state beneficiaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_in_New_Zealand) and pensioners, receive less than \$24,000.[^cite_note-276] Moreover, [child poverty in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_poverty_in_New_Zealand) has been identified by the government as a major societal issue;[^cite_note-Chpov-277][^cite_note-278] the country has 12.0% of children living in low-income households that have less than 50% of the median equivalised disposable household income as of June 2022.[^cite_note-279] Poverty has a disproportionately high effect in ethnic-minority households, with a quarter (23.3%) of Māori children and almost a third (28.6%) of Pacific Islander children living in poverty as of 2020.[^cite_note-Chpov-277]&#x20;

### Trade

New Zealand is heavily dependent on international trade,[^cite_note-280] particularly in agricultural products.[^cite_note-281] Exports account for 24% of its output,[^cite_note-CIA-102] making New Zealand vulnerable to international commodity prices and global [economic slowdowns](25382.md). Food products made up 55% of the value of all the country's exports in 2014; wood was the second largest earner (7%).[^cite_note-282] [New Zealand's main trading partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_New_Zealand), as at June 2018, are China (NZ\$27.8b), Australia (\$26.2b), the European Union (\$22.9b), the United States (\$17.6b), and Japan (\$8.4b).[^cite_note-GSTC_StatNZ-283] On 7 April 2008, New Zealand and China signed the [New Zealand–China Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand%E2%80%93China_Free_Trade_Agreement), the first such agreement China has signed with a developed country.[^cite_note-284] In July 2023, New Zealand and the European Union entered into the [EU–New Zealand Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand%E2%80%93European_Union_relations#EU-NZ_free_trade_agreement), which eliminated tariffs on several goods traded between the two regions.[^cite_note-285] This free trade agreement expanded on the pre-existing free trade agreement[^cite_note-286] and saw a reduction in tariffs on meat and dairy[^cite_note-287] in response to feedback from the affected industries.[^cite_note-288]&#x20;

The service sector is the largest sector in the economy, followed by manufacturing and construction and then farming and raw material extraction.[^cite_note-CIA-102] [Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_New_Zealand) plays a significant role in the economy, contributing \$12.9 billion (or 5.6%) to New Zealand's total GDP and supporting 7.5% of the total workforce in 2016.[^cite_note-TourismStats-289]&#x20;

A Romney ewe with her two lambs

Wool has historically been one of New Zealand's major exports.

Wool was New Zealand's major agricultural export during the late 19th century.[^cite_note-RWT_export_evolution-251] Even as late as the 1960s it made up over a third of all export revenues,[^cite_note-RWT_export_evolution-251] but since then its price has steadily dropped relative to other commodities,[^cite_note-290] and wool is no longer profitable for many farmers.[^cite_note-291] In contrast, [dairy farming](24443459.md) increased, with the number of dairy cows doubling between 1990 and 2007,[^cite_note-292] to become New Zealand's largest export earner.[^cite_note-293] In the year to June 2018, dairy products accounted for 17.7% (\$14.1 billion) of total exports,[^cite_note-GSTC_StatNZ-283] and the country's largest company, [Fonterra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fonterra), controls almost one-third of the international dairy trade.[^cite_note-294] Other exports in 2017–18 were meat (8.8%), wood and wood products (6.2%), fruit (3.6%), machinery (2.2%) and wine (2.1%).[^cite_note-GSTC_StatNZ-283] [New Zealand's wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_wine) industry has followed a similar trend to dairy, the number of vineyards doubling over the same period,[^cite_note-295] overtaking wool exports for the first time in 2007.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297]&#x20;

### Infrastructure

In 2025, [renewable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_New_Zealand) reached a new record, generating 47.7% of [New Zealand's gross energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_New_Zealand) supply, up from 45.6% in 2024.[^cite_note-298] The majority of the [country's electricity supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_New_Zealand) is generated from [hydroelectric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_power_in_New_Zealand), with major schemes on the [Waikato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waikato_River), [Waitaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitaki_River) and [Clutha / Mata-Au](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clutha_River_/_Mata-Au) rivers, as well as at [Manapouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manapouri_Power_Station). [Geothermal power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_power_in_New_Zealand) is also a significant generator of electricity, with several large stations located across the [Taupō Volcanic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taup%C5%8D_Volcanic_Zone) in the North Island. The four main companies in the generation and retail market are [Contact Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contact_Energy), [Genesis Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genesis_Energy_Limited), [Mercury Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_Energy) and [Meridian Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_Energy). State-owned [Transpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpower_New_Zealand) operates the high-voltage transmission grids in the North and South Islands, as well as the [Inter-Island HVDC link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HVDC_Inter-Island) connecting the two together.[^cite_note-Energy2015-299]&#x20;

The provision of [water supply and sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_New_Zealand) is generally of good quality. Regional authorities provide water abstraction, treatment and distribution infrastructure to most developed areas.[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301]&#x20;

A mid-size jet airliner in flight. The plane livery is all-black and features a New Zealand silver fern mark.

A [Boeing 787-9 Dreamliner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_787_Dreamliner) of [Air New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_New_Zealand), the flag carrier of New Zealand

[New Zealand's transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_New_Zealand) network comprises 94,000 kilometres (58,410 mi) of roads, including 199 kilometres (124 mi) of motorways,[^cite_note-302] and 4,128 kilometres (2,565 mi) of railway lines.[^cite_note-CIA-102] Most major cities and towns are linked by bus services, although the private car is the predominant mode of transport.[^cite_note-303] The [country's railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_New_Zealand) were privatised in 1993 but were re-nationalised by the government in stages between 2004 and 2008. The state-owned enterprise [KiwiRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KiwiRail) now operates the railways, with the exception of commuter services in Auckland and Wellington, which are operated by [Auckland One Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_One_Rail) and [Transdev Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transdev_Wellington) respectively.[^cite_note-304] Railways run the length of the country, although most lines now carry freight rather than passengers.[^cite_note-305] The road and rail networks in the two main islands are linked by [roll-on/roll-off](1457484.md) ferries between Wellington and [Picton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picton,_New_Zealand), operated by [Interislander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interislander) (part of KiwiRail) and [Bluebridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/StraitNZ). Most international visitors arrive via air.[^cite_note-306] New Zealand has [four international airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_New_Zealand): [Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Airport), [Christchurch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch_Airport), [Queenstown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queenstown_Airport) and [Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Airport). Only Auckland and Christchurch have runways long enough for aircraft capable of long haul flights to Asia and the Americas.[^cite_note-307]&#x20;

The [New Zealand Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Post_Office) had a monopoly over [telecommunications in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_New_Zealand) until 1987 when [Telecom New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spark_New_Zealand) was formed, initially as a state-owned enterprise and then privatised in 1990.[^cite_note-Wilson2010-308] [Chorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chorus_Limited), which was split from Telecom (now Spark) in 2011,[^cite_note-309] still owns the majority of the telecommunications infrastructure, but competition from other providers has increased.[^cite_note-Wilson2010-308] A large-scale rollout of gigabit-capable [fibre to the premises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibre_to_the_premises), branded as [Ultra-Fast Broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra-Fast_Broadband), began in 2009 with a target of being available to 87% of the population by 2022.[^cite_note-310] As of 2017, the United Nations [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union) ranks New Zealand 13th in the development of information and communications infrastructure.[^cite_note-311]&#x20;

### Science and technology



New Zealand physicist [Ernest Rutherford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Rutherford) has been described as "the father of nuclear physics".

The accumulation of knowledge of agricultural practice and the effects of herbal remedies in the treatment of illness and disease by Māori *[tohunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tohunga)* as well as their precise observation of nature might be considered an indigenous contribution to science in New Zealand. [Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook)'s voyages in the 1700s and [Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin)'s in 1835 had important scientific botanical and zoological objectives.[^cite_note-Science_New_Zealand-312] The establishment of universities in the 19th century fostered scientific discoveries by notable New Zealanders including [Ernest Rutherford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Rutherford) for splitting the atom, [William Pickering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Pickering_\(rocket_scientist\)) for rocket science, [Maurice Wilkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Wilkins) for helping discover DNA, [Beatrice Tinsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatrice_Tinsley) for galaxy formation, [Archibald McIndoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archibald_McIndoe) for plastic surgery, [Sydney Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Smith_\(pathologist\)) for forensics and [Alan MacDiarmid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_MacDiarmid) for conducting polymers.[^cite_note-New_Zealand_Herald-313]&#x20;

[Crown Research Institutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Research_Institute) (CRIs) were formed in 1992 from existing government-owned research organisations. Their role is to research and develop new science, knowledge, products and services across the economic, environmental, social and cultural spectrum for the benefit of New Zealand.[^cite_note-314] The total gross expenditure on [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) (R\&D) as a proportion of GDP rose to 1.37% in 2018, up from 1.23% in 2015. New Zealand ranks 21st in the OECD for its gross R\&D spending as a percentage of GDP.[^cite_note-315] New Zealand was ranked 26th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317]&#x20;

The [New Zealand Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Space_Agency) was created by the government in 2016 for space policy, regulation and sector development. [Rocket Lab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Lab) was the first commercial rocket launcher in the country.[^cite_note-318]&#x20;

Most private and commercial research organisations in New Zealand focus on the agricultural and fisheries sectors. Examples include the [Cawthron Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cawthron_Institute), the [Livestock Improvement Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livestock_Improvement_Corporation), the [Fonterra Research and Development Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fonterra#Fonterra_Research_and_Development_Centre), the [Bragato Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Winegrowers#Bragato_Research_Institute), the [Kiwifruit Breeding Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zespri#Kiwifruit_Breeding_Centre), and [B+LNZ Genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef_%2B_Lamb_New_Zealand#B+LNZ_Genetics).&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_New_Zealand) and [List of cities in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_New_Zealand)

Stationary population pyramid broken down into 21 age ranges.

[Population pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_pyramid) (2017)

The [2023 New Zealand census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_census) enumerated a resident population of 4,993,923, an increase of 6.3% over the [2018 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_New_Zealand_census) figure.[^cite_note-Census2023PopCounts-5] As of October 2026, the total population has risen to an estimated 5,357,820.[^cite_note-populationestimate-14] New Zealand's population increased at a rate of 1.9% per year in the seven years ended June 2020. In September 2020 [Statistics New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics_New_Zealand) reported that the population had climbed above 5 million people in September 2019, according to population estimates based on the 2018 census.[^cite_note-population1-319][^cite_note-321]&#x20;

New Zealand's population today is concentrated to the north of the country, with around 76.4% of the population living in the North Island and 23.6% in the South Island as of June 2025.[^cite_note-NZ_population_data_2018-322] During the 20th century, New Zealand's population [drifted north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drift_to_the_north). In 1921, the country's [median centre of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_population) was in the Tasman Sea west of [Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levin,_New_Zealand) in [Manawatū-Whanganui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manawat%C5%AB-Whanganui); by 2017, it had moved 280 km (170 mi) north to near [Kawhia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawhia_Harbour) in Waikato.[^cite_note-323]&#x20;

New Zealand is a predominantly urban country, with 84.5% of the population living in [urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_areas_of_New_Zealand) and 51.4% in the seven cities with populations exceeding 100,000.[^cite_note-NZ_population_data_2018-322] [Auckland](18660332.md), with over 1.4 million residents, is by far the largest city.[^cite_note-NZ_population_data_2018-322] New Zealand cities generally rank highly on international livability measures. For instance, in 2024, Auckland was ranked the world's fifth [most liveable city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most_liveable_city) and Wellington the fourteenth by the Mercer Quality of Living Survey.[^cite_note-324]&#x20;

The [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) of the New Zealand population at the 2023 census was 38.1 years, up from 37.4 years in the 2018 census[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] with life expectancy in 2022–2024 being 80.1 years for males and 83.5 years for females.[^cite_note-327] While New Zealand is experiencing [sub-replacement fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility), with a total fertility rate of 1.6 in 2020, the fertility rate is above the OECD average.[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] By 2050, the median age is projected to rise to 43 years and the percentage of people 60 years of age and older to rise from 18% to 29%.[^cite_note-UNtwspop-330]&#x20;
