### Ethnicity and immigration

Main articles: [New Zealanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealanders) and [Immigration to New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_New_Zealand)

Pedestrians crossing a wide street which is flanked by storefronts

Pedestrians on [Queen Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Street,_Auckland) in Auckland, an ethnically diverse city

In the [2023 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_census), 62.1% of residents responded as being ethnically [New Zealand European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_New_Zealanders), and 17.8% responded as being ethnically [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people). Other major ethnicities include [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_New_Zealanders) (5.2%), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_New_Zealanders) (5.1%), [Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_New_Zealanders) (4.3%), [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_New_Zealanders) (2.2%), [Tongan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongan_New_Zealanders) (2.0%), [Cook Islands Maori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islanders) (1.9%), and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_New_Zealanders) (1.4%)[^cite_note-Census2023PopCounts-5] 13.2% responded with multiple ethnicities, with 8.2% alone responding as both European and Māori.[^cite_note-331] Statistics New Zealand allows people to choose multiple ethnicities and reports ethnic data based on total responses, therefore ethnicities add up to more than 100%. For legacy systems which can only accept one ethnicity, people are usually allocated to a single ethnicity based on a pre-determined priority order, with Māori given highest priority and New Zealand European given lowest priority.[^cite_note-\:02-332]&#x20;

The population has become more multicultural and diverse in recent decades: in 1961, the census reported that the population of New Zealand was 92% European and 7% Māori, with Asian and Pacific minorities sharing the remaining 1%.[^cite_note-333] However, New Zealand's non-European population is disproportionately concentrated in the North Island and especially in the [Auckland Region](676368.md): while Auckland is home to 33% of New Zealand's population, it is home to 62% of the country's Pasifika population and 60% of its Asian population.[^cite_note-Census2023PopCounts-5]&#x20;



[Pasifika Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_Festival) celebrating [Pasifika New Zealanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders) and their pan-cultural identity

While the [demonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym) for a New Zealand citizen is New Zealander, the informal "[Kiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwi_\(nickname\))" is commonly used both internationally[^cite_note-334] and by locals.[^cite_note-335] The Māori loanword *[Pākehā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81keh%C4%81)* has been used to refer to [New Zealanders of European descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_New_Zealanders), although some reject this name. The word today is increasingly used to refer to all non-Polynesian New Zealanders.[^cite_note-Pakeha-336]&#x20;

The Māori were the first people to reach New Zealand, followed by the early [European settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81keh%C4%81_settlers). Following colonisation, immigrants were predominantly from Britain, Ireland and Australia because of restrictive policies similar to the [White Australia policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Australia_policy).[^cite_note-337] There was also significant [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_people), [Dalmatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalmatia),[^cite_note-338] German, and Italian immigration, together with indirect European immigration through Australia, North America, South America and South Africa.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-Immigration_history-340] Net migration increased after the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War); in the 1970s and 1980s policies on immigration were relaxed, and immigration from Asia was promoted.[^cite_note-Immigration_history-340][^cite_note-341] In 2009–10, an annual target of 45,000–50,000 permanent residence approvals was set by the New Zealand Immigration Service—more than one new migrant for every 100 New Zealand residents.[^cite_note-Recent_immigration-342]&#x20;

In the 2023 census, 28.8% of people counted were not born in New Zealand, up from 27.4% in the 2018 census. The most popular countries of origin for overseas-born residents were England (14.6%), mainland China (10.2%), India (10.0%), the Philippines (7.0%), South Africa (6.7%), Australia (6.1%), Fiji (4.8%) and Samoa (4.3%).[^cite_note-343] The number of fee-paying [international students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_student) and [international exchange students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_exchange_program) increased sharply in the late 1990s, with more than 20,000 studying in public [tertiary institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) in 2002.[^cite_note-344] New Zealand achieved a record net migration gain of 135,500 people in the year ended October 2023.[^cite_note-345] Immigrants from India, China and the Philippines are the largest contributors to New Zealand's population growth.[^cite_note-346]&#x20;

In 2025, New Zealand's net migration fell to its lowest level in more than a decade. According to Statistics New Zealand, the country recorded a net migration gain of 14,200 in 2025, down 9,600 from a year earlier. That marked the smallest annual increase since 2013, outside 2021, when borders were largely closed during the COVID-19 outbreak.[^cite_note-347]&#x20;

### Language

Main article: [Languages of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_New_Zealand)

Map of New Zealand showing the percentage of people in each census area unit who speak Māori. Areas of the North Island exhibit the highest Māori proficiency.

Speakers of Māori according to the 2013 census:

[^cite_note-348]

&#x20; Less than 5%

&#x20; More than 5%

&#x20; More than 10%

&#x20; More than 20%

&#x20; More than 30%

&#x20; More than 40%

&#x20; More than 50%

English is the predominant language in New Zealand, spoken by 95.4% of the population.[^cite_note-Census2018_pdc-349] [New Zealand English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_English) has a distinctive [accent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accent_\(sociolinguistics\)) and [lexicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicon).[^cite_note-350] It is similar to [Australian English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_English), and many speakers from the Northern Hemisphere are unable to tell the accents apart.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHayMaclaganGordon200814-351] The most prominent differences between New Zealand English and other English dialects are the shifts in the short front vowels: the short-*i* sound (as in *kit*) has centralised towards the [schwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwa) sound (the *a* in *comma* and *about*); the short-*e* sound (as in *dress*) has moved towards the short-*i* sound; and the short-*a* sound (as in *trap*) has moved to the short-*e* sound.[^cite_note-352]&#x20;

After World War II, Māori were discouraged or prohibited to speak their own language (*[te reo Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_reo_M%C4%81ori)*) in schools and workplaces, and it existed as a community language only in a few remote areas.[^cite_note-Māori_language-353] The Native Schools Act 1867 required instruction in English in all schools, and while no official policy banned children from speaking Māori, many suffered [physical abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_abuse) if they did.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] The Māori language has recently undergone a process of revitalisation,[^cite_note-357] being declared one of New Zealand's official languages in 1987,[^cite_note-358] and is spoken by 4.0% of the population.[^cite_note-Census2018_pdc-349][^cite_note-360] Māori-language immersion schools and two Māori-language television channels now exist.[^cite_note-361] [Many places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_dual_place_names_in_New_Zealand) have both their Māori and English names officially recognised.[^cite_note-362]&#x20;

As recorded in the 2018 census,[^cite_note-Census2018_pdc-349] [Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_language) is the most widely spoken non-official language (2.2%), followed by "Northern Chinese" (including [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese), 2.0%), [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi) (1.5%), and French (1.2%). [New Zealand Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Sign_Language) was reported to be understood by 22,986 people (0.5%); it became one of New Zealand's official languages in 2006.[^cite_note-363]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_New_Zealand)



The [Wellington Cathedral of St Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Cathedral_of_St_Paul) in [Wellington](33804.md)

According to the 2023 census, 51.6% of the "census usually resident population" had [no religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion),[^cite_note-\:1-364] up from 48.2% in 2018 census.[^cite_note-Census2018_pdc-349] [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_New_Zealand) are the single largest religious group, forming 32.3% of the population,[^cite_note-\:1-364] compared to 36.5% in 2018.[^cite_note-Census2018_pdc-349] [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_New_Zealand) are the second largest religious minority, forming the 2.9% of population, followed by [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_New_Zealand) on 1.5%.[^cite_note-\:1-364] The Auckland Region exhibited the greatest religious diversity.[^cite_note-365]&#x20;

### Education

Main articles: [Education in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_New_Zealand) and [Tertiary education in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education_in_New_Zealand)

Primary and secondary schooling is compulsory for children aged 6 to 16, with the majority of children attending from the age of 5.[^cite_note-Education_Stats-366] There are 13 school years and attending [state (public) schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school) is free to New Zealand citizens and permanent residents from a person's 5th birthday to the end of the calendar year following their 19th birthday.[^cite_note-367] New Zealand has an adult literacy rate of 99%,[^cite_note-CIA-102] and over half of the population aged 15 to 29 hold a tertiary qualification.[^cite_note-Education_Stats-366]&#x20;

There are five types of government-owned tertiary institutions: universities, colleges of education, [polytechnics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_technology#New_Zealand), specialist colleges, and [wānanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C4%81nanga),[^cite_note-EducationAct1989-368] in addition to private training establishments.[^cite_note-NZQA-369] In 2021, in the population aged 25–64, 13% had no formal qualification, 21% had a school qualification, 28% had a tertiary certificate or diploma, and 35% have a bachelor's degree or higher.[^cite_note-370] The [OECD](33853117.md)'s [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) ranks New Zealand as the 28th best in the OECD for maths, 13th best for science, and 11th best for reading.[^cite_note-371]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_New_Zealand)

See also: [Healthcare in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_New_Zealand) and [Te Whatu Ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Whatu_Ora)

As of 2023, New Zealand allocated about 9.6 percent of its GDP to healthcare, slightly above the OECD average of 8.8 percent. The public healthcare system operates under a publicly funded health insurance model that supports inpatient and outpatient services at public hospitals, along with preventive and primary care through private providers.[^cite_note-372] *Health New Zealand – Te Whatu Ora* is the primary [publicly funded healthcare system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_healthcare) of New Zealand. The New Zealand Government established it to replace the country's 20 [district health boards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_health_board) (DHBs) on 1 July 2022. Health New Zealand is charged with working alongside the [Public Health Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_Agency_\(New_Zealand\)) to manage the provision of [healthcare services in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-Stuff_21_April_2021-373][^cite_note-RNZ_health_shake-up-374] As of 2022, the agency is New Zealand's largest employer, consolidating the DHBs' combined work force of 80,000, with an estimated annual operating budget of NZ\$20 billion and an asset base of about NZ\$24 billion.[^cite_note-DPMC_interim_boards-375] Ischaemic heart disease is the leading cause of death, followed by Alzheimer disease and other dementias, and stroke. The life expectancy has improved from 78.6 years in 2000 to 82.2 years in 2021.[^cite_note-376]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Zealand)

Tall wooden carving showing Kupe above two tentacled sea creatures&#x20;

Late 20th-century house-post depicting the navigator [Kupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kupe) fighting two sea creatures

Statistics have shown around 20% of New Zealanders identify with more than one ethnic group. Around 80% are of European descent, 15% Maori, and others coming from China, the Pacific Islands and elsewhere. This means there is little sense in talking of one New Zealand culture, although, according to one writer, "New Zealanders will argue strongly for the existence of a unique identity, for the existence of something that sets them apart from others, a "Kiwi culture"."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKennedy2007397–398-377]&#x20;

The British and Irish immigrants brought aspects of their own culture to New Zealand and also influenced Māori culture,[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379] particularly with the introduction of Christianity.[^cite_note-380] However, Māori still regard their allegiance to tribal groups as a vital part of [their identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_identity), and Māori kinship roles resemble [those of other Polynesian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_kinship).[^cite_note-381] More recently, [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States), [Australian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Australia), [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Asia) and other [European cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe) have exerted influence on New Zealand. Non-Māori Polynesian cultures are also apparent, with [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_Festival), the world's largest Polynesian festival, now an annual event in Auckland.[^cite_note-382]&#x20;

The largely rural life in early New Zealand led to the image of New Zealanders as rugged, industrious problem solvers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKennedy2007400-383] Modesty was expected and enforced through the "[tall poppy syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tall_poppy_syndrome)", where high achievers received harsh criticism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKennedy2007399-384] At the time, New Zealand was not known as an intellectual country.[^cite_note-385] From the early 20th century until the late 1960s, Māori culture was suppressed by the attempted [assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) of Māori into British New Zealanders.[^cite_note-Māori_language-353] In the 1960s, as tertiary education became more available, and [cities expanded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization)[^cite_note-386] urban culture began to dominate,[^cite_note-387] but rural imagery and themes are common in New Zealand's art, literature and media.[^cite_note-388]&#x20;

[New Zealand's national symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_New_Zealand) are influenced by natural, historical, and Māori sources. The [silver fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_fern) emblem appears on army insignia and sporting team uniforms.[^cite_note-Wilson2016-389] Certain items of popular culture thought to be unique to New Zealand are called "[Kiwiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwiana)".[^cite_note-Wilson2016-389]&#x20;

### Art

Main article: [New Zealand art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_art)

Refer to caption

Portrait of Hinepare of [Ngāti Kahungunu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Kahungunu) by [Gottfried Lindauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottfried_Lindauer), showing chin [moko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81_moko), [pounamu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounamu) [hei-tiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hei-tiki) and woven cloak

As part of the resurgence of Māori culture, the traditional crafts of carving and weaving are now more widely practised, and Māori artists are increasing in number and influence.[^cite_note-Creative-390] Most Māori carvings feature human figures, generally with three fingers and either a natural-looking, detailed head or a stylised version.[^cite_note-391] Surface patterns consisting of spirals, ridges, notches and fish scales decorate most carvings.[^cite_note-392] The pre-eminent Māori architecture consisted of carved meeting houses (*[wharenui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharenui)*) decorated with symbolic carvings and illustrations. These buildings were originally designed to be constantly rebuilt, changing and adapting to different whims or needs.[^cite_note-393]&#x20;

Māori decorated the white wood of buildings, canoes and cenotaphs using red (a mixture of red [ochre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ochre) and shark fat) and black (made from soot) paint and painted pictures of birds, reptiles and other designs on cave walls.[^cite_note-394] Māori tattoos (*[moko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81_moko)*) consisting of coloured soot mixed with gum were cut into the flesh with a bone chisel.[^cite_note-395] Since European arrival paintings and photographs have been dominated by landscapes, originally not as works of art but as factual portrayals of New Zealand.[^cite_note-beginnings-396] Portraits of Māori were also common, with early painters often portraying them as an ideal race untainted by civilisation.[^cite_note-beginnings-396] The country's isolation delayed the influence of European artistic trends allowing local artists to develop their own distinctive style of [regionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_\(art\)).[^cite_note-397] During the 1960s and 1970s, many artists combined traditional Māori and Western techniques, creating unique art forms.[^cite_note-398] New Zealand art and craft has gradually achieved an international audience, with exhibitions in the [Venice Biennale](301876.md) in 2001 and the "Paradise Now" exhibition in New York in 2004.[^cite_note-Creative-390][^cite_note-399]&#x20;

Māori cloaks are made of fine flax fibre and patterned with black, red and white triangles, diamonds and other geometric shapes.[^cite_note-400] [Greenstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounamu) was fashioned into earrings and necklaces, with the most well-known design being the [hei-tiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hei-tiki), a distorted human figure sitting cross-legged with its head tilted to the side.[^cite_note-401] Europeans brought English fashion etiquette to New Zealand, and until the 1950s most people dressed up for social occasions.[^cite_note-402] Standards have since relaxed and New Zealand fashion has received a reputation for being casual, practical and lacklustre.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-The_Economist_print_edition-404] However, the local fashion industry has grown significantly since 2000, doubling exports and increasing from a handful to about 50 established labels, with some labels gaining international recognition.[^cite_note-The_Economist_print_edition-404]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [New Zealand literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_literature)

Māori quickly adopted writing as a means of sharing ideas, and many of their oral stories and poems were converted to the written form.[^cite_note-405] Most early English literature was obtained from Britain, and it was not until the 1950s when local publishing outlets increased that New Zealand literature started to become widely known.[^cite_note-406] Although still largely influenced by global trends ([modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_modernism)) and events (the Great Depression), writers in the 1930s began to develop stories increasingly focused on their experiences in New Zealand. During this period, literature changed from a [journalistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalism) activity to a more academic pursuit.[^cite_note-407] Participation in the world wars gave some New Zealand writers a new perspective on New Zealand culture and with the postwar expansion of universities local literature flourished.[^cite_note-408] Dunedin is a UNESCO [City of Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Literature).[^cite_note-409]&#x20;

### Media and entertainment

Main articles: [Music of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_New_Zealand), [Cinema of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_New_Zealand), and [Media of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_New_Zealand)



[Lorde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorde) is one of the most internationally successful New Zealand artists.

New Zealand music has been influenced by [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues), [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music), [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll) and [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop_music), with many of these genres given a unique New Zealand interpretation.[^cite_note-music-410] Māori developed a varied [musical tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_music) around songs and chants, including ceremonial performances, laments, and love songs.[^cite_note-411] Instruments ([taonga pūoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taonga_p%C5%ABoro)), such as flutes and percussion, began being used as spiritual tools, entertainment, and signalling devices.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413] Early settlers brought over their ethnic music, with [brass bands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brass_band) and [choral music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choir) being popular, and musicians began touring New Zealand in the 1860s.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415] [Pipe bands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pipe_band) became widespread during the early 20th century.[^cite_note-416] The New Zealand recording industry began to develop from 1940 onwards, and many New Zealand musicians have obtained success in Britain and the United States.[^cite_note-music-410] Some artists release Māori language songs, and the Māori tradition-based art of *[kapa haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapa_haka)* (song and dance) has made a resurgence.[^cite_note-417] The [New Zealand Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Music_Awards) are held annually by [Recorded Music NZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recorded_Music_NZ); the awards were first held in 1965 by [Reckitt & Colman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reckitt_%26_Colman) as the [Loxene Golden Disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loxene_Golden_Disc) awards.[^cite_note-418] Recorded Music NZ also publishes the country's [official weekly record charts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_New_Zealand_Music_Chart).[^cite_note-419]&#x20;

Hills with inset, round doors. Reflected in water.

The [Hobbiton Movie Set](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hobbiton_Movie_Set), located near [Matamata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matamata), was used for [*The Lord of the Rings* film trilogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings_\(film_series\)).

[^cite_note-420]

Public [radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_in_New_Zealand) was introduced in New Zealand in 1922.[^cite_note-421] A state-owned [television service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_New_Zealand) began in 1960.[^cite_note-422] Deregulation in the 1980s saw a sudden increase in the numbers of radio and television stations.[^cite_note-NZ_TV-423] New Zealand television primarily broadcasts American and British programming, along with many Australian and local shows.[^cite_note-424] The number of [New Zealand films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Zealand_films) significantly increased during the 1970s. In 1978 the [New Zealand Film Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Film_Commission) started assisting local film-makers, and many films attained a world audience, some receiving international acknowledgement.[^cite_note-NZ_TV-423] The highest-grossing New Zealand films are *[Hunt for the Wilderpeople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunt_for_the_Wilderpeople)*, *[Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_\(2010_film\))*, *[The World's Fastest Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World%27s_Fastest_Indian)*, *[Whale Rider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whale_Rider)*, *[Once Were Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Once_Were_Warriors_\(film\))*, [Tinā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin%C4%81), *[Heavenly Creatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavenly_Creatures)*, *[What We Do in the Shadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/What_We_Do_in_the_Shadows)* and *[The Piano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Piano)*.[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427] The country's diverse scenery and compact size, plus government incentives,[^cite_note-428] have encouraged some producers to shoot very big-budget and well known productions in New Zealand, including *[The Lord of the Rings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings_\(film_series\))* and *[The Hobbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hobbit_\(film_series\))* film trilogies, *[Avatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatar_\(2009_film\))*, *[The Chronicles of Narnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chronicles_of_Narnia_\(film_series\))*, *[King Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Kong_\(2005_film\))*, *[Wolverine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-Men_Origins:_Wolverine)*, *[The Last Samurai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Last_Samurai)*, *[The Power of the Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Power_of_the_Dog_\(film\))*, *[Alien Covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien:_Covenant)*, *[Mulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulan_\(2020_film\))*, and *[A Minecraft Movie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Minecraft_Movie)*.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431] The New Zealand media industry is dominated by a small number of companies, most of which are foreign-owned, although the [state retains ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_entity) of some television and radio stations.[^cite_note-432] Since 1994, [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) has consistently ranked New Zealand's press freedom in the top twenty, with the 19th freest media as of 2015.[^cite_note-433]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [New Zealand cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_cuisine)

Raw meat and vegetables

Ingredients to be prepared for a [hāngī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C4%81ng%C4%AB)

The national cuisine has been described as "[Pacific Rim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Rim), with food drawing inspiration from Europe, Asia and Polynesia". There is a wide choice offered in restaurants throughout the country.[^cite_note-cuisine-434] A distinctive feature of New Zealand cuisine is the casual way it is eaten, reflecting the country's laid-back lifestyle. Summertime is popular for eating out in cafes or at a barbeque, where a range of meat and seafood is readily available. A tradition Maori [hangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangi), where food is cooked underground beside heated rocks, is popular, usually prepared for special occasions.[^cite_note-cuisine-434] [Fish and chips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_and_chips) is a national favorite takeaway.[^cite_note-cuisine-434]&#x20;

New Zealand yields produce from land and sea—most crops and livestock, such as maize, potatoes and pigs, were gradually introduced by the early European settlers.[^cite_note-435] Distinctive ingredients or dishes include [lamb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamb_and_mutton), salmon, *[kōura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dura)* (crayfish),[^cite_note-436] [Bluff oysters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluff_oyster), [whitebait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitebait), *[pāua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81ua)* (abalone), mussels, scallops, *[pipi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paphies_australis)* and *[tuatua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuatua)* (types of New Zealand shellfish),[^cite_note-437] *[kūmara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%ABmara)* (sweet potato), [kiwifruit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwifruit), [tamarillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamarillo), and [pavlova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlova_\(cake\)) (considered a national dessert).[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-cuisine-434] A [hāngī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C4%81ng%C4%AB) is a traditional Māori method of cooking food using heated rocks buried in a pit oven that is still used for large groups on special occasions,[^cite_note-439] such as *[tangihanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangihanga)*.[^cite_note-440]&#x20;

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_New_Zealand)

Rugby team wearing all black, facing the camera, knees bent, and facing toward a team wearing white

A [haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka_\(sports\)) performed by the [New Zealand national rugby union team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_rugby_union_team) ("All Blacks") before a game. The haka is a challenge with vigorous movements and stamping of the feet.

Most of the major sporting codes played in New Zealand have British origins.[^cite_note-441] [Rugby union](25405.md) is considered the [national sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_sport)[^cite_note-442] and attracts the most spectators.[^cite_note-Organised_Sport-443] [Golf](19568112.md), [netball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netball), [tennis](29773.md) and [cricket](25675557.md) have the highest rates of adult participation, while netball, rugby union and [football (soccer)](10568.md) are particularly popular among young people.[^cite_note-Organised_Sport-443][^cite_note-nzsssc-444] [Horse racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thoroughbred_racing_in_New_Zealand) is one of the most popular [spectator sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectator_sport) in New Zealand and was part of the "rugby, racing, and beer" subculture during the 1960s.[^cite_note-445] Around 54% of New Zealand adolescents participate in sports for their school.[^cite_note-nzsssc-444] Victorious rugby tours to Australia and the United Kingdom in the [late 1880s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1888%E2%80%9389_New_Zealand_Native_football_team) and the [early 1900s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Original_All_Blacks) played an early role in instilling a national identity.[^cite_note-446] Māori participation in European sports was particularly evident in rugby, and the country's team performs a [haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka_\(sports\)), a traditional Māori challenge, before international matches.[^cite_note-447] New Zealand is known for its [extreme sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_sport), [adventure tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventure_travel)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain200669-448] and strong [mountaineering](20341.md) tradition, as seen in the success of notable New Zealander [Sir Edmund Hillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Hillary) summiting [Mount Everest](42179.md) first.[^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451] Other outdoor pursuits such as [cycling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycling_in_New_Zealand), fishing, swimming, running, [tramping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tramping_in_New_Zealand), [canoeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canoeing), hunting, snowsports, surfing and sailing are also popular.[^cite_note-SportsParticipation-452] New Zealand has seen regular sailing success in the [America's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America%27s_Cup) regatta since 1995.[^cite_note-453] The Polynesian sport of [waka ama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_ama) racing has experienced a resurgence of interest in New Zealand since the 1980s.[^cite_note-454]&#x20;

New Zealand has competitive international teams in [rugby union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_rugby_union_team), [rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_rugby_league_team), [netball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_netball_team), [cricket](478115.md), [softball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_men%27s_national_softball_team), and [sailing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_New_Zealand). New Zealand participated at the [Summer Olympics](27566.md) in 1908 and 1912 as [a joint team with Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasia_at_the_Olympics), before first participating [on its own](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_at_the_Olympics) in 1920.[^cite_note-455] The country has ranked highly on a medals-to-population ratio at recent Games.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457][^cite_note-458] The [All Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_rugby_union_team), the national rugby union team, are the most successful in the history of international rugby.[^cite_note-459] They have won the [Men's Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup) three times.[^cite_note-460]&#x20;

New Zealand is ranked fifth in the [International Cricket Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cricket_Council) Men's Test Team Rankings 2025 with a rating of 100.[^cite_note-461]&#x20;

Originally the Pakeha were the early European settlers, however, today 'Pakeha' is used to describe any peoples of non-Maori or non-Polynesian heritage. Pakeha is not an ethnicity but rather a way to differentiate between the historical origins of our settlers, the Polynesians and the Europeans, the Maori and the other.
